People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what brings me in is that I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to actually finish them. I mean, even important work stuff.

I do computer support, and I’ll be tracing a client’s network problem, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries or rereading them over and over, and I still don’t really know what they said. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I’m trying, but my mind just… doesn’t stay with it. Does that make sense?

Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.

If I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, R41.0, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what brings me in is that I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to actually finish them. I mean, even important work stuff.\n\nI do computer support, and I’ll be tracing a client’s network problem, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries or rereading them over and over, and I still don’t really know what they said. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I’m trying, but my mind just… doesn’t stay with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.\n\nIf I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.

Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.

The clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.

So… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.\n\nIf I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.

The fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. That’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.

Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.

I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.\n\nIf I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.\n\nMy reputation’s taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role — and that’s put stress on us at home. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.\n\nMy reputation’s taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role — and that’s put stress on us at home. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it hasn’t just been one place.\n\nAt work, or with work-related things especially, it’s shown up a lot more since I lost the structure of my old job. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. And then at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people don’t trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s been across work and home. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention and impulsivity during your teenage years as well?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t remember it as clearly as my mother does.\n\nShe still jokes that even back in grade school my report cards would say I was bright, but that I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of wrote that off as just being a kid, or being enthusiastic, but… I guess looking back, it was there pretty early.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it hasn’t just been one place.\n\nAt work, or with work-related things especially, it’s shown up a lot more since I lost the structure of my old job. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. I always kind of wrote that off as just being a kid, or being enthusiastic, but… I guess looking back, it was there pretty early.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to you avoiding social situations or relationships due to feelings of embarrassment or frustration?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I avoid relationships, exactly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

I do sometimes feel embarrassed if I’ve been scattered or said something too quickly, and I might hesitate a bit before seeing people if I’m worried I’ll come across badly. But I don’t know that I’ve really cut people off or stopped having relationships because of it.

Maybe I’m not answering that quite right — is that what you mean?

It’s embarrassing, honestly. I’m trying, but my mind just… doesn’t stay with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.\n\nIf I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Um… I guess what brings me in is that I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to actually finish them. I mean, even important work stuff.\n\nI do computer support, and I’ll be tracing a client’s network problem, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries or rereading them over and over, and I still don’t really know what they said. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I’m trying, but my mind just… doesn’t stay with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.\n\nIf I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.\n\nMy reputation’s taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role — and that’s put stress on us at home. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it hasn’t just been one place.\n\nAt work, or with work-related things especially, it’s shown up a lot more since I lost the structure of my old job. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. And then at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people don’t trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s been across work and home. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention and impulsivity during your teenage years as well?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t remember it as clearly as my mother does.\n\nShe still jokes that even back in grade school my report cards would say I was bright, but that I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and lost homework even when I’d actually done it. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.\n\nMy reputation’s taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role — and that’s put stress on us at home. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention and impulsivity during your teenage years as well?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t remember it as clearly as my mother does.\n\nShe still jokes that even back in grade school my report cards would say I was bright, but that I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of wrote that off as just being a kid, or being enthusiastic, but… I guess looking back, it was there pretty early.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to you avoiding social situations or relationships due to feelings of embarrassment or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid relationships, exactly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI do sometimes feel embarrassed if I’ve been scattered or said something too quickly, and I might hesitate a bit before seeing people if I’m worried I’ll come across badly. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it hasn’t just been one place.\n\nAt work, or with work-related things especially, it’s shown up a lot more since I lost the structure of my old job. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. Is that a reasonable way to put it?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present for as long as you can remember, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"My mother still jokes that in grade school my report cards said I was bright but never stayed in my seat, blurted out answers, and lost homework even when I had done it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s been there a long time… probably since I was a kid, honestly.

My mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — they’d say I was bright, but that I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I don’t know how much to read into childhood report cards, but it does make me think this didn’t just start recently.

Does that answer what you’re asking?

I mean, even important work stuff.\n\nI do computer support, and I’ll be tracing a client’s network problem, and then I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries or rereading them over and over, and I still don’t really know what they said. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I’m trying, but my mind just… doesn’t stay with it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or fidgety when you're trying to focus on your work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, out of control or anything, but I do notice it.\n\nIf I’m sitting with a client, or even in a chess meeting, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.\n\nMy reputation’s taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role — and that’s put stress on us at home. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it hasn’t just been one place.\n\nAt work, or with work-related things especially, it’s shown up a lot more since I lost the structure of my old job. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. And then at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people don’t trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s been across work and home. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention and impulsivity during your teenage years as well?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t remember it as clearly as my mother does.\n\nShe still jokes that even back in grade school my report cards would say I was bright, but that I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of wrote that off as just being a kid, or being enthusiastic, but… I guess looking back, it was there pretty early.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to you avoiding social situations or relationships due to feelings of embarrassment or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid relationships, exactly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI do sometimes feel embarrassed if I’ve been scattered or said something too quickly, and I might hesitate a bit before seeing people if I’m worried I’ll come across badly. But I don’t know that I’ve really cut people off or stopped having relationships because of it.\n\nMaybe I’m not answering that quite right — is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to any significant problems with your daily life or relationships that you feel are impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I’d have to say yes, even if I don’t love admitting that.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder to keep together, and it’s affected how people see me. My reputation has taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I also missed out on a senior service-manager role. That was… pretty discouraging.\n\nAnd at home, it’s put pressure on my wife and me financially, because people don’t trust me to follow through the way they used to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it has definitely affected my daily life and how I feel about myself. Is that the kind of impact you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been relatively consistent across different situations and environments?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yes, though I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to it in that exact way.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like it’s only one place or one situation, if that’s what you mean. I’d like to say quiet environments are easier, and they probably are in some ways… but honestly, it doesn’t take much to pull me off track.

I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Sometimes I’ll have a little cable tie or something in my fingers and just keep turning it over and over until someone gives me that look, like… “can you stop?” So, yes, I guess I do get fidgety when I’m trying to sit still and focus.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I did okay in school, so I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but my mother still jokes about my grade school report cards.\n\nThey’d say things like I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which… sounds kind of familiar still. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. I try not to, and I know it’s rude, but especially in tech conversations, the answer will kind of jump into my head before the other person is done.\n\nLike if someone’s explaining a network issue, I’ll start talking over them, or I’ll reach for the keyboard to “just check something” before they’ve finished describing the problem. I don’t mean it as disrespectful. In my head I’m thinking I’m helping, but… yeah, I can see how it comes across. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers in social situations, like when talking to friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m constantly barging into conversations, but yes, it happens.\n\nWith friends or family it’s maybe a little less “professional sounding,” but the same thing—someone will be in the middle of explaining something, and I’ll think I know where they’re going, so I jump in before they’re finished. In tech conversations especially, the answer just sort of pops into my head and I start talking over them, or if I’m at the keyboard I’ll want to grab it and check something before they’ve even finished telling me what’s wrong.\n\nI know better, and I do try to stop myself. But… yeah, I cut people off more than I’d like. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in your daily life, like doing things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so, though I don’t think of myself as reckless exactly. It’s more like in the moment my brain jumps ahead and I act before I’ve fully paused.\n\nThe clearest example is conversations, especially tech stuff. Someone will be explaining a problem, and I’ll think I already know the answer, so I cut in or start reaching for the keyboard before they’re even done. I know better, and I’m not trying to be rude, but I sort of move faster than my filter sometimes.\n\nSo… yes, if that counts as acting on impulse, then I’d say it happens more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they worsened over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there pretty much my whole life, honestly. I don’t think it’s something that just showed up recently.\n\nMy mother still jokes about my grade school report cards — that they’d say I was bright, but I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and somehow lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of laughed that off as just being a busy kid, but… looking back, it does sound pretty consistent.\n\nIs that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d like to say it’s just annoying little stuff, but honestly it has caused real problems.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I think I could hide it better, or at least the system around me helped keep me on track. Since losing that structure, my reputation has taken a pretty big hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because people didn’t feel they could trust me to follow through, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I think I might’ve been qualified for otherwise.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and I have definitely had to worry about money more than we should, partly because of that. It’s embarrassing to say, because I don’t want it to sound like I just don’t care or I’m not trying. But yes, it’s affected relationships and daily life in a significant way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt overwhelmed by your difficulties with attention and impulsivity to the point where you felt really upset or distressed?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I know everyone gets frustrated with themselves sometimes.\n\nBut there have definitely been times where I’ve felt really worn down by it — like I’m trying hard, and still things slip or I react too quickly, and then I feel embarrassed or disappointed in myself afterward. It can get pretty upsetting, especially when I can see the consequences and I know I should have handled it better.\n\nSo… yes, distressed is fair. Maybe not something I’m proud to admit, but fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying focused and paying attention are more problematic for you than your fidgetiness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the focus part is the bigger problem for me, honestly.\n\nThe fidgetiness and impulsive stuff can be embarrassing, sure, but what really brings me in is that I just can’t seem to keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it’s important. Like I’ll be trying to trace a client’s network problem, and ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a bit, if that makes sense. It’s not just restlessness — it’s like I can’t hold onto the thread.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships, such as with your wife or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, especially with my wife. I try not to put it all on this, because I know I’m responsible for following through, but since I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder.\n\nMy reputation’s taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role — and that’s put stress on us at home. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because people don’t really trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo, yes… I guess that does count as relationship problems, doesn’t it? It’s hard to say out loud, but it’s affected us.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one area stand out as more troublesome than the others?\n\nPatient: I’d say the attention part stands out the most, if that’s a reasonable way to put it.\n\nThe restlessness and acting too quickly can be a problem, but what really brings me in is that I can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to finish it anymore, even when it matters. Like I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve reread the same log entries three times and still don’t actually know what they say.\n\nThat’s the part that feels most troublesome to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present in multiple settings, such as at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it hasn’t just been one place.\n\nAt work, or with work-related things especially, it’s shown up a lot more since I lost the structure of my old job. My reputation has taken a pretty big hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role. And then at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people don’t trust me to follow through the way they used to.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s been across work and home. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention and impulsivity during your teenage years as well?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… though I don’t remember it as clearly as my mother does.\n\nShe still jokes that even back in grade school my report cards would say I was bright, but that I didn’t stay in my seat, blurted out answers, and lost homework even when I’d actually done it. I always kind of wrote that off as just being a kid, or being enthusiastic, but… I guess looking back, it was there pretty early.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to you avoiding social situations or relationships due to feelings of embarrassment or frustration?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid relationships, exactly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI do sometimes feel embarrassed if I’ve been scattered or said something too quickly, and I might hesitate a bit before seeing people if I’m worried I’ll come across badly. But I don’t know that I’ve really cut people off or stopped having relationships because of it.\n\nMaybe I’m not answering that quite right — is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and impulsivity led to any significant problems with your daily life or relationships that you feel are impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I’d have to say yes, even if I don’t love admitting that.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, things have gotten harder to keep together, and it’s affected how people see me. My reputation has taken a real hit — I’ve lost freelance clients, and I also missed out on a senior service-manager role. That was… pretty discouraging.\n\nAnd at home, it’s put pressure on my wife and me financially, because people don’t trust me to follow through the way they used to. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, but it has definitely affected my daily life and how I feel about myself. Is that the kind of impact you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been relatively consistent across different situations and environments?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, yes, though I’m not sure I’ve paid attention to it in that exact way.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like it’s only one place or one situation, if that’s what you mean. I’ve noticed versions of it at work, at home, and when I’m dealing with people socially. But I also don’t want to overstate it — some environments make it easier to manage than others, especially if there’s a lot of structure.\n\nSo I guess my answer is that it’s been fairly consistent, but the amount it shows depends on the situation.
